# Decisions and history (archive)

Not loaded automatically. This is the full record behind the rules in
`CLAUDE.md`, `DESIGN.md` and `STANDARD.md`: why each rule exists, what was
reported, what was measured. Search it before changing an area
(`grep -n "<area>" docs/DECISIONS.md`). New history is added here, one entry
per change; the rule itself goes in exactly one loaded file.

Part 1 is `CLAUDE.md` and part 2 is `STANDARD.md` §24 (Project Memory), both
verbatim as they stood on 2026-09-26. `DESIGN.md` as it stood is in
`docs/DESIGN-NOTES.md`.

---

# Part 1. CLAUDE.md as of 2026-09-26

# ADspace Digital Portal — working rules

@DESIGN.md
@STANDARD.md

Static site, vanilla JS, no build step, served by GitHub Pages at
digital.adspace.me (CNAME in the repo). Pages: `admin/` (console),
`creators/` (client-facing creator selection), `review/` (client-facing
content review). Supabase behind `js/api.js`; schema in
`supabase/schema.sql` (re-runnable; the user runs it by hand in the SQL
editor, and must be told when). A change confined to one function or one
column ships instead as a dated file in `supabase/migrations/`, narrowly
scoped and safe to run twice, so a live database is not put through fifteen
unrelated data migrations to replace one function body.

The design system, the components, the laws and the copy rules are in
`DESIGN.md`. The general engineering and design standard, the working method
and the completion gate are in `STANDARD.md`, whose Project Memory records the
three places this portal deliberately departs from it (the spacing scale, the
14px console body, and the 1280/390 viewport matrix). Both are imported above. Read all
three before any change; where they disagree, `STANDARD.md` §2 settles it and
this portal's documented behaviour wins.

## 1. Verified code engine

### Locked features (merged to `main`, PRs #3 to #52)

| Area | Locked behaviour |
|---|---|
| Theme (`css/portal.css`, `js/admin.js`) | Light is the default everywhere and never taken from `prefers-color-scheme`. Dark is the console's alone, chosen from a toggle in the sidebar foot above Sign out, kept in `localStorage` as `adspace-theme`, applied by a head script before first paint, and hung off `:root[data-theme="dark"]`. `/client/`, `/creators/` and `/review/` never carry the attribute, so a client never decides on a proposal in a register nobody chose. |
| Screen typeface | System stack for everything except the display sizes, which take Slate Regular through `--font-head` (`/css/SlateRg.woff2`, 38KB, `font-display: swap`; the TTF stays for pdf-lib and as the fallback). Optima is the letterhead wordmark and stays out of the portal. |
| The wordmark keeps its proportions (`css/portal.css` `.brand-logo`, `index.html`, `404.html`, 2026-09-21) | **`height` plus `width: auto` plus the global `img { max-width: 100% }` is a squash waiting for a narrow box.** The width is clamped to what is left and the height is not, so the mark is compressed horizontally and the wordmark stops being the wordmark — reported by the user on 2026-09-21. Measured in the 244px console rail, where the box settles at 195px: every stand-in past **8.13:1 drew at 8.13:1**, ratio 10 and ratio 14 landing on the same squashed figure, and the wider the asset the worse it got; the top bar has room at 1280 and would fail the same way in a narrower one. The fix cannot depend on the asset's intrinsic ratio — `brandLogo` is served from the CDN, which the sandbox's network policy blocks, and a new file can be uploaded any day — so it is **`object-fit: contain` with `object-position: left center`**: the ink fits whatever box the layout gives it with the ratio true and sits on the rail's own margin instead of centred in the leftover width. The box still measures its stated height, so nothing under it moves; what gives is the drawn height of a mark too wide for the rail. The two root pages take the same two declarations on `.doorbar img`. Measured with stand-ins of stated ratios (6, 8, 10, 12, 14) before and after, because the mechanism does not depend on knowing the real one. |
| The sign-in page holds still (`js/chrome.js`, `css/portal.css`, `admin/index.html`, 2026-09-26) | **Reported by the user: the header moved on load, and on a phone the mark was missing.** Measured before the fix: the mark lands from the CDN after the first paint and an image with no file has no width, so the page label jumped **208px** right when it arrived; the footer was added at `DOMContentLoaded`, so the sign-in card, centred between the bar and the floor, moved **26px** up when the floor rose; and the page was grey until the session was read, then white. Now: **the mark's proportions are kept from the last visit** (`adspace-logo-ratio` in `localStorage`, written on load) and the box is drawn at that size from the first paint (`aspect-ratio: auto R`, replaced by the file's own ratio once it loads); **on a first visit the brand is held back** (`.brand.is-waiting`, `visibility: hidden`) until the mark loads or fails, at most 2.5s, so it arrives once, in place. `ADspaceChrome.mark()` (the console rail) reserves the same way. **The footer is drawn with the header** and sits last on the screen from the first paint (`.portalfoot { order: 1 }` in the body's flex column), then is moved to the end of the body once the page is parsed, so the keyboard and a screen reader reach it last; `body:has(> .console:not([hidden])) > .portalfoot` hides it while the footer still precedes the console. **The first paint knows whether a session is kept** (`data-boot` from the head script, off an `sb-*-auth-token` key; `js/admin.js` `gate()` removes it, and so does a 4s timeout): signed out is white from the start, signed in draws no sign-in bar or footer for a frame over the console about to open. **The phone mark**: `.topbar .brand-logo` is `flex: 0 0 auto; max-width: 60vw`, because Safari resolves the global `img { max-width: 100% }` against a `.brand` that is sized by its own content and, on a phone where the label beside the mark is hidden, drew the mark 0px wide; a `vw` cap needs no container. Verified in Chromium by measuring the bar every frame through load at 1280 and 390, first visit and second; **Safari itself cannot be run in the sandbox**, so the phone mark is fixed on the mechanism and is the user's to confirm. **The bar and the foot run to the screen's edges on every page** (`.topbar-inner, .portalfoot-in { max-width: none }`; the user, 2026-09-26: the header "still moves and center-aligned … when the browser gets larger"): the sign-in page was the last to keep the centred 1240px box, so its mark slid across as the window widened; it now sits at x=24 at every width, where the rail's mark sits once the console opens. |
| The rail's activity glyphs (`js/crm.js` `SECTION_ICON` / `logIcon`, 2026-09-21) | **A section with no glyph draws the bare dot, which reads as a fault beside rows that have one.** `SECTION_ICON` was missing `register` (Documents) and `ops` (My Work), and `logIcon` tested `/^doc\./` where every tag this portal writes is `document.*` or `register.*` — so a letter issued against a client drew a hollow circle in Recent activity. Found by **reading** the walk screenshot the first time the Activity pane was seeded with rows: the fault had been on the screen since the block shipped, and no screenshot anywhere had ever contained a row to show it. That seed is now in the walk (`tests/uxaudit.js`, three rows on the Laman Citra record, names long enough to exercise the `By` column and a detail long enough to wrap), because a pane that walks empty is a pane whose one row shape nobody has measured. |
| No profile-image placeholders (`js/crm.js`, `js/campaigns.js`, `js/ops.js`, `css/portal.css`, 2026-09-21) | **A mark holds artwork or it is not a mark.** Two monogram discs survived the creators list's removal because neither sat on a list of creators: `.log-avatar`, a 28px disc of one initial beside the actor on every row of the client's and the campaign's Activity panes, and `.bcard-face`, a 20px initials disc beside the owner on every board card with a **dashed empty one** where there was no owner. Both stand in for a profile picture this portal holds none of and is not going to — no avatar exists anywhere in the data model — so each drew the same grey circle down every row, and where the letter did vary it said nothing the full name beside it had not already said. A dashed empty circle standing for an unassigned task is a placeholder for a placeholder. The log row is `.log-who` > `.log-person` and the board card's foot is the owner's name; `actorInitial()` is gone from both files. **What stays and why**: `.rec-mark` holds the client's own `logo_url` and falls back to initials only while we wait for one, and `.mk-avatar` carries that logo into a platform's own UI on the review mockups — both hold real artwork. Every other circle in the stylesheet was checked in the same pass and is a real logo (`.bigcard-logo`, `.logopreview`), a glyph (`.ovlog-ico`), a control (`.iconbtn` and its kin) or a platform mockup's own furniture. The global Activity record (`js/admin.js`) already drew its actor as plain text. |
| Header and footer (`js/chrome.js`) | One chrome on every page: mark from `brandLogo`, kicker from `data-kicker`, actions from `data-actions` (`lang`, `qr`); "Digital Portal" and "Logged in as {email}" in the console; outlined Sign out with icon. Footer left `© {year} ADSPACE PLT. All Rights Reserved.`, right `Terms of Service` → https://adspacestudios.com/legal/policies; footer on the floor on short pages. |
| Full-page states | One `.cover` component for no link, bad link, access code, closed, confirmed, nothing to review, access denied; same words on `creators/` and `review/`. |
| Content Review (`review/`, `js/review.js`, `js/mockups.js`) | Post mockups per platform (Instagram feed 4:5 1080×1350, Reels and Stories 9:16 at cover ratio, TikTok, Facebook feed and carousel, rednote); Facebook carousel: 2 tiles side by side, 4 as 2×2, 5 as 2 above 3 with `+N` greyed on the fifth; handle shown on the mockup, not the client name; client logo fills the circle with no border or shadow; placeholder avatar is an illuminance gradient, never grey or rainbow; Approve needs a name and Approved reads outlined with Request changes hidden; Copywriting label, copy text control at the top; the Instagram send glyph is the one concave dart Instagram draws, not Feather's `send` (a quadrilateral plus a separate crease from the same apex: at 22px with a round cap the two strokes pile up at that corner and the mark reads as a smudge), because a mockup is only a preview while every mark in it is the one the client's audience sees; **the caption block is spaced the way the app spaces it**: Save hangs off the far right of the action row (`.mk-act-save`, `margin-left: auto`) and never sits against Share, the `more` toggle rides the end of the truncated line over a short fade (`.mk-clampwrap.is-clamped`, both ends of the width stated so it animates) rather than taking a row of its own, and a blank line between paragraphs is collapsed **while the caption is clamped** (`.mk-clamp br + br`), because `<br><br>` spent one of the two preview lines on nothing and printed the ellipsis on an empty row; likes, caption and comments are one block of text, so the three gaps that separate them are the same 4px; no Save as PDF; og:title `{client name} Content Review Portal by ADspace` on `review/` only. |
| Console: Content Review | Content sets folded, one open at a time; Publish / Unpublish (warn); resend for approval with a note; Google Drive import with a progress bar; S3 upload by signed PUT; only active clients listed. **Deleting a content set is `can_remove`, and the icon is drawn behind it** (`body.no-remove #deleteSet`): it takes the set's posts and their approval records with it and there is no restore, so it is the same authority as a client, a letter, a contact or a short link, and it was the one hard delete in the console whose control was drawn for everybody. A Marketing group without the permission was offered a trash icon that could not work. **A delete that removes nothing is not a success**: `db.from('batches').delete()` and `db.from('posts').delete()` both reported one. PostgREST answers a refused delete with 204 and **no error at all** — the row simply stays — so the set panel closed, nothing was said, and the set was still in the list underneath; the post delete was worse and threw its answer away entirely. Both take `.select('id')` now and name the refusal (`Not deleted. The database refused the request.`) rather than repainting a list that has not changed. `tests/setdel.js` drives all three states and `tests/stub2.js` can refuse a delete the way RLS does (`window.__refuseDelete = { batches: true }`), because a stand-in that cannot say no is one that cannot test a refusal. |
| Console: Clients (`js/crm.js`) | **One set of handles per client, not two.** The Brand profile held `social_*` and Content Review's client settings held `handle_*`, over the same row, so a handle corrected on the record left the one printed on the client's own mockup untouched. Brand now edits the `handle_*` columns the review page reads and the mockups print, and carries the **client logo** (`logo_url`) with the same disc preview, which is also the record's mark. `social_*` is backfilled by `handle_of()` — a bare handle as typed, a pasted profile URL's last segment, and **nothing at all where that segment is a route** (`instagram.com/p/DXyz` would otherwise print `DXyz` on a client's post) — and is never written again. The links row derives each address from the handle per platform (`profileUrl()`), because `https://` + `@starliving` is nothing. **Delete client** is in the record's ⋯, behind `can_remove` (`body.no-remove #crmClientMenuWrap`), a sheet that counts what goes from the record already loaded and takes the client's name typed back, plus the delete code where `delete_code_set()` says one exists; `delete_client` re-checks `allowed('remove')` when the button is pressed. Paused and Past remain how a client leaves the working list; this is for the lead keyed in twice. Lead intake (Brand name, the trading name; the registered company name is Billing's; Source, Contact person, phone, email, Enquiry; Owner, Industry, Market); record in sale order; contacts as a table; Billing details fold with ring summary and billing contact from Contacts; Brand profile fold; Calls and visits with next actions and Undo; a stage clock (`stage_since`, `stage_log`, stamped by the `clients_stage_clock` trigger) showing time in the current stage under the chip on the list and the whole journey as one line on the record, with `client.stage` as its own activity tag; ageing off `STALE_H` (Lead 48 hours, Proposal sent 21 days, calendar days, Contacted unset) marking an over-run stage "2 days · Overdue" in warn and counting them in the group head; Services lines (qty × rate × months from a start date, Enquired / To quote / Confirmed, prefilled not fixed); Documents (the Letter of Offer); Engagements only once Active; stage select on the head with the Active gate. **Urgency to commence** (`clients.commence`: 1 to 3 months / 3 to 6 months / 6 months or more, blank until asked) on the intake form and the record, because the stage clock measures how long we have taken and not how long the client will wait. **The enquiry is edited like every other fact about the client**: it used to sit in the intake-only block with the contact fields, so it could be typed once at the door and never again; the contact fields stay at intake, because after that a person is a row in Contacts. **On a phone the row is a two column table**, not a card with the chip pushed right: name over meta on the left, the state chip over the age in a **fixed 120px track** on the right (measured against its worst case, "18 months · Overdue" at 119px), both top aligned. The meta line carries the deal value where there is one and omits what is not known, never a bare currency sign standing in for a figure. **Three bands, in the order somebody works them**: **Leads** first (speed to first contact is the number that moves conversion, and a lead under a hundred and eighty clients is a lead nobody rings), **Clients** (active *and* paused — a paused client is still a client and reads wrong filed under an ending, so the working book of business is one thing to scan), and **Past clients** (ended engagements, **shut by default** and remembered, because nobody opens this page to read one; they keep their place and their count rather than disappearing, since they are still clients, still hold their number, and may renew). A band is a `<button>` carrying its count, so it clears the control floor (`--ctl-h-sm`) like every other control — `.svc-cat`'s own padding left it at 42px and `uxaudit`'s `target` rule is what said so. **A filter opens every band**, because somebody who searched for a name wants the row wherever it is and a shut band would hide the one match and say nothing. Each band draws its first thirty with `Show N more` under it. **The Client ID is on the row**, under the name, in the token face: a seventh column would cost the name its width on every row for a number only read with an invoice in hand, and the phone template has nowhere to put it; it is searched alongside the two names. The ID itself is unchanged — manual, `^[A-Z0-9]{2,12}$`, unique, and load bearing, because the Letter of Offer serial is `AQL/{client_code}/{YYMM}{SEQ}`. **The list is a card per stage**, drawn by `js/group.js` like every other directory (`ADspaceGroup.section`: `.crm-group` > `.crm-group-head` + `.crm-group-body` > `.crm-table.softpanel.crm-register`): Leads, Clients and Past clients each under its own 15px heading carrying the count and the overdue mark, each table with its own header row, the stage's name the fold (`.crm-group-fold`), which opens and shuts the card in place. It was one surface with the stages as uppercase `.svc-cat.crm-band` divider rows for a week; the user sent that back on 2026-09-22 (the eyebrow face and its spacing read as wrong) and asked for the cards the rate card uses for Services and Add-ons, so the Register is drawn the same way (a card per family, newest first, a sort in the bar). Columns are Client, Stage, Industry, Person in charge, **Last activity** (no Value column and no value on a heading, 2026-09-26: what a client is worth is read on the record's Services; a card's rows run **newest Client ID first**, digits compared as numbers, unnumbered records after them newest first) (`loadLastSeen()`: the newest of a logged call or visit, the stage moving (`stage_since`, `Added` while it has never moved) and a document issued (a Letter of Offer or a Documents row), the date over a small mute word saying which, the user's choice of 2026-09-26; each read is one the record already makes, a refused one is left out rather than failing the column, and a date ahead of now is a plan and never counts) and the chevron in the column the header leaves empty. **The row is one `<button>`** with nothing nested in it, so the whole of it opens the workspace from a pointer and from the keyboard alike. Every state is a screen somebody lands on and every one is driven by `tests/register.js`: populated, loading, empty, filtered empty and refused, at 1280 and 390. **The empty states are `ADspaceState.emptyLine`**, not a private copy of the same markup. **A filter repaints only when the filter changed**: `input` and `change` both fire for one keystroke and the second arrives on **blur**, so pressing Clear the filters blurred the search box, repainted the list, and detached the button between mousedown and click — the one way out of a filtered empty list did nothing at all when it was clicked with a mouse, and a test that dispatched the event instead of clicking it could not see that. |
| Console: Clients, the record's identity and Overview (`js/crm.js` `paintIdentity` / `paintSummary`) | **The identity is two parts, and they centre on each other.** Left: the mark, then `.rec-who` (the name with its stage select on one line, the meta line under them). Right: `.rec-ctl`, one ⋯ holding **Edit** and **Delete client** (the delete item alone carries `data-need="clients:manage"`; the ⋯ itself draws for everybody). The campaign head has the same shape with **Edit details** in its ⋯. Edit used to sit beside the stage on every record, and on a phone it and the ⋯ wrapped to a line of their own under the name with the meta below them, which is the wrong reading order and the loosest part of the card spent on its rarest act; and on a desktop the controls sat on the name line, above the card's centre on every record with a meta line. `.rec-id` is `auto minmax(0, 1fr) auto` with `align-items: center`, so a long name wraps under itself and the state follows it, and the ⋯ stays on the middle of the card at every width. **On a phone the two parts are two rows** (`.is-narrow .rec-id`: `"mark . ctl" / "who who who"`): the 38px mark at the top left, the state chip and the ⋯ at the top right, then who this is under them full width on the card's own margin. Beside the mark the name had 130px, a campaign's chip wrapped under it, and the campaign's purpose line, drawn outside the grid, sat on a different margin from the name and the meta. The campaign's `.camp-purpose` is the second of three lines inside `.rec-who` (name, purpose, client · person in charge) and its chip lives in `.rec-ctl` beside the ⋯ at every width. `tests/six.js` measures the ⋯ against the card's centre at 1280, and at 390 that the mark and the ⋯ share the top row and who this is sits under them on the mark's margin; both widths assert Edit is in the menu. **The campaign's state chip is centred on the ⋯ beside it** (`.rec-ctl .chip { align-self: center }`): the shared `.chip` rule pins a chip to the top of its flex row, so the state sat 11px above the control's centre and read as squeezed in beside the dots rather than on the same line; `tests/six.js` measures the two centres at 1280 and 390. **The rate card row is the same height with or without its ⋯** (`.is-narrow .cat-row > .team-act` takes the roster's negative block margin): the ⋯ draws for an admin only, and its 44px target had made the same row 97px on an admin's phone and 68px on everybody else's. **The client's stage select is in `.rec-ctl` beside the ⋯**, the shape the campaign head already had: on the name line it stood mid line on a phone with nothing to its right. **Below 1100 the rail splits around the panes**: `.rec:not(.cportal) > .rec-rail` is `display: contents` and its blocks take `order`, so Account status, Next action, the billing gate and Profile come before the tab strip and the three `.rail-after` blocks (Key dates, Details, Recent activity) follow the pane; one rail above the tabs had put the record's own content a screen and a half down on a phone. The client portal is excluded, because its contact rows key on the rail's own width class and a rail that is not a box has none. `tests/six.js` asserts the select's centre against the ⋯ at both widths and the block order at 390. **Key dates draws no rule under nothing** (`.raildates:empty + .rail-date-edit`): every date row is omitted when the record does not hold it, so a client with none showed a heading, a gap and a hairline over an empty list. Its Save is an outlined `.btn-sm` centred on the field (`align-items: center`), not a bare word bottom-aligned against a box of another height. **The record opens on an identity area, not a title strip.** `.rec-id` carries a 46px mark — the client's own `logo_url` where we hold one, their initials where we do not (`initialsOf()`: two characters of a Chinese name, the first letters of the first two **letter-initial** words otherwise, so Dale & Cecil is DC and not "D&") — the name, the stage select, Edit, and a meta line naming the main contact's preferred language and the person in charge, each omitted when it is not known. The disc stays light in both themes for the reason the review mockups do: a client's logo is their artwork and is not ours to invert. **The Overview is an operational record**: one `.ovcard` surface of flat titled sections divided by hairlines — Contact details, Services, Letters, Calls and visits — each a heading, the one control that opens its pane (`Edit` with the pen on Contact details, `Manage services ›`, `View all ›`), and concise real rows (`.ovfacts` as label beside value in a 150px track, 104px when narrow, never eyebrow-over-value two across; `.ovtable` with stated tracks; `.ovlog` with a glyph per kind of call). Built **only from what the record has already loaded** (contacts, service lines, documents, touches): no second read, no stored number, no invented metric, no new field, RPC, permission or workflow. Enquired service lines are not shown, for the reason the letter leaves them out. A section with nothing says so in one line rather than disappearing, because "None issued." is itself an answer. **The rail is one block per question** (`.railblock`, each with a `.railtitle` at the pane's own 15px section size, not an eyebrow): **Account status** (the stage chip and the stage clock, `12 days in this stage`, with `· Overdue` in warn where `isStale()` says so; Active for left Key dates when this arrived, so the clock is stated once), Next action, the **billing gate** (`.railgate`: a red `--err` block, drawn only while a billing field is missing on a record not yet Active, a button that opens Billing where `maySeeBilling()`, plain otherwise; it was an amber `.notice` reading "Not active." on every lead, which is a caution's colour on a refusal), Profile (the completion bar), Key dates, Details, Recent activity. Key dates and Recent activity rows carry a neutral 16px glyph (`RICON` / `ico()` in `js/crm.js`: calendar, phone, pin, person, file, tag, image, speaker, link), and the Overview's calls do too, in a 30px `--sunk` disc. **Next action prefers the one a person wrote** (`client_touches.next_action` / `next_at`, overdue in warn) over the step `nextStep()` derives, because a person decided it and a derivation did not. **Record completion is a bar with the missing list under it**, never a bare figure, counted over billing, brand, contacts and services. **Key dates omit a row whose date is not there** (Client since from `created_at`, Last contact and Next follow up from touches). **Recent activity is the last three `activity_log` rows**, read once when the record opens and kept in `state.log`, so the Activity pane no longer re-reads. **A block leaves entirely when its data does not exist**, so the rule under the last one is a class `paintRail` sets: `:last-child` counts a hidden sibling. Person in charge is on the identity line and Added is in Key dates, so neither is repeated in Details. **`.rec` states `grid-template-rows: auto 1fr`**: the rail spans both rows, so with `auto auto` a rail taller than tabs plus pane had its extra shared between them and a lead opened with sixty pixels of nothing between its tab strip and its first section. |
| The client record's Timeline (`js/crm.js` `railDates` / `journeyOf`, 2026-09-21) | **Every date and every duration in one block, and the durations are the point.** Asked for by the user on 2026-09-21: the record stated its durations in three places and none of them answered the question the team actually has. `Leads 10 days · Active today` sat as a mute line under Details, `Active · Since today` in the rail's Account status, and Key dates held only `Client since` — so "how long does a lead wait to be contacted" could not be read anywhere. **Key dates is Timeline**, because a block holding `9 days` is not a block of dates: the stage journey is a row per stage above a rule (the stage, when it began, how long it ran) and the dates the record holds are the rows below it. **Each stage row is one of the turnarounds a report will average later** — time in Lead is speed to first contact, time in Contacted is contact to proposal, time in Proposal sent is how long it took to close, time in Active is how long this client has been engaged — and **nothing new is stored for any of it**: `stage_log` has been stamped by the `clients_stage_clock` trigger since it shipped, so the averages across every client need no field, no migration and no backfill. **The stage that is running carries the weight and the overdue mark** (`.tl-row.is-now`, `isStale()`), which is why **the Account status block is gone**: the stage select in the head says where the record stands and this says for how long, and a rail block repeating the head's own control was the same fact twice. That supersedes the 2026-09-21 decision that "the rail opens on Account status (stage chip plus stage clock)". **Client since is edited where it sits** (`ADspaceAsk.rename` with `type: 'date'` and `value`, `#crmSinceVal` + `#crmSinceEdit`): the value on the row becomes the picker and the pen beside it becomes the tick, so the separate labelled field and Save button under the list are gone — the shape `js/ask.js` already gave every value already on the screen, extended once so a date can seed its field with `2026-06-01` while the row reads `12 Aug 2026`. The save takes `.select('id')` and names a refusal, like every other write a policy can refuse. **The row is two tracks and not three**: a name, a date and a duration are all `nowrap`, and three of them measured at exactly 365px in a 365px rail — no slack at all, so the first longer stage name pushed the page sideways, which `uxaudit`'s `overflow` rule caught on the first run. The duration ends the row on its own margin and the date rides mute beside the name. The pen is `--ctl-h` with the rate card's negative block margin, because the audit's control floor is 38px and `--ctl-h-sm` is for `.btn-sm` and its kin. **Every stage date is editable too** (2026-09-25, the user: a client keyed in today who has been a client since April read "Lead, today"): each stage row carries its own pen beside its duration, the stage moves still fill the dates in, **Client since is the first stage's start** so moving one moves the other, a stage may not begin before the one before it and no date is in the future, and one save writes `stage_log`, `stage_since` (the last entry) and `created_at` together. The clock trigger only stamps a date when the stage itself changes, so no migration was needed. `tests/crm.js` drives the journey rows, the once-only rule, the absent Account status block, the picker opening seeded, Escape putting the row back, the pen saving and the activity row; `tests/stub2.js`'s client fixtures gained the `created_at` the real NOT NULL column always has, without which the block drew no dated rows at all. |
| Directories (`js/group.js`) | **Every console directory is one shape, and the shape is a card per group.** `ADspaceGroup.section({ route, key, name, count, marks, shut, table })` draws the 15px heading with its count and marks, the name as the fold, and the card with its own header row (`ADspaceGroup.table(rowClass, heads)`), with `ADspaceGroup.more()` drawing thirty rows and offering the rest; **once Show more is pressed the card keeps every row for as long as the page is open** (2026-09-26, the user: a save on Documents put the rows back behind Show more), known by the section drawing it, so no caller names it. **Show more's hover is `--line-soft`**: it was `--fill`, the ink surface, under ink text, so hovering drew a black bar with no words (the user, 2026-09-26). Clients by stage, Content Review one card of clients, Creator Campaigns by state (Completed shut), the Creators List by fee band (Inactive shut), Short Links Live and Paused (Paused shut), Documents by family, Services a card per category, Team a card per user group. A fold is remembered per browser under `adspace-groups` by route and key; a filter opens every card; **a card that holds everything on the page never shuts by default** (`lone`), or a route with every link paused was a heading over nothing. **No tracked uppercase labels anywhere in the console** (2026-09-22): every table header, band, fact label, step title and the sidebar kicker is 12px, 600, mute, sentence case with no letter-spacing; the empty-state card takes `--card-line` like every card, and the rail's action rows (Next action, the profile's missing list) keep an edge one tone lighter (`--line-soft`) because they open something, while the billing gate keeps its red. **The card carries no outline, on one token** (`--card-line`, transparent, shared by `.panel` (the record head and every form), `.crm-table`, `.softpanel`, `.team-table`, `.ovcard`, `.bookreg`; the Team card's local `border: 0` is gone): the user chose that look on 2026-09-22 and asked why one card could differ at all, which a local exception is the answer to. Its ground against the page is the edge in both themes, and the 1px border stays transparent so nothing shifts. **The card opens and shuts in place** (`.crm-group-body` runs `grid-template-rows` 0fr to 1fr with the opacity, at `--t-slow` / `--ease`, and empties itself once shut), never by repainting the directory: a fold is a move somebody made and the eye follows a surface that arrives. The user asked for this on 2026-09-22 after the seven routes had four different shapes (cards on Clients and the Register, uppercase `.svc-cat.crm-band` divider rows on Campaigns, the Creators List, Services and Team, and a bare card on Content Review and Short Links), and for folds rather than tabs on a long directory, because a folded card costs one line and still says how many it holds where a tab hides the count. **The Campaigns / Creators strip** (`.tabrow`) sits at the page top with the bar one block gap (12px) under it, so the search on Campaigns opens where it opens on every other route; it stood 18px down with 4px under it. A global search across the console is deferred: the section search filters a list in place, which a jump-to cannot, and the jump is worth building once the lists are in the hundreds. The campaign Overview's Schedule rows state a 176px shoot track, measured against `22 Sept 2026, 12:30pm` (at 142 the time wrapped under the date). The Schedule pane's Upcoming / Past rows and the letter's line picker keep `.svc-cat`: they are dividers inside a record pane, not a directory. |
| Operations (`supabase/migrations/2026-09-19-operations-system.sql`, `docs/OPERATIONS-SETUP.md`) | **The monthly task spreadsheet, as a system.** Fourteen `ops_` tables, two seeded workflows (general and video) with their stages as rows, six task templates, and twenty-three security-definer functions. **Every important write is a function, and the tables carry a select policy and nothing else**: a browser cannot set a stage, move a commitment, assign a person, stamp a completion or file an event, whatever it sends. The one direct write a page may make is marking its own notification read. **Row level security is stated one table at a time, never inside a `do $$` loop**: the Supabase SQL editor scans a script for the literal `alter table … enable row level security` lines and offers to append its own where it cannot find them, so fourteen tables enabled correctly by `execute format(...)` still put a warning in front of the person running the file, and a posture a reader cannot see in the file is one nobody can check. The loop keeps only the policy drop, which is read from `pg_policies` and not from a list. **Permissions are the portal's ladder, not seven new booleans** — two authorisation systems is a security defect, not a style question, because a policy would have to ask both and the day they disagree the database answers one way and the page another. `ops` View / Work / Manage is use, create, assign; `ops.all`, `ops.reports`, `ops.workflows` and `ops.time` are the team queue, the reports, the templates and another person's hours. **Those four are granted, never inherited** (`ops_granted()`, which reads the exact key and does not fall back): every part before them was a pane *inside* its section's job, so falling back to the section was right; these four are *more* than "work my own tasks", and inherited, a group given `{"ops":"work"}` would read every colleague's queue until an admin remembered to opt them out, and a group added next year would start there again. Silence means no. **The original commitment is written once and never again**, so a report can see replanning; a date move takes a reason category and files the old and new value. **Three different durations and never one number**: cycle time from the events, stage time from the events, and active work only from a session somebody started. Nothing records idle time, keystrokes, screens or location. One open session a person across every task, enforced by a partial unique index as well as by the function; one accountable owner a task, the same way; an ended assignment is kept, because reassignment is not deletion. Stage gates are data, not opinion (Ready needs an owner and a date, Editing is refused while footage is not ready, Client review needs a draft link, Delivered needs a final link, Done needs a delivery or a stated reason). **Retry safe where a second press is plausible**: creating, revising and generating a month all key on what makes them the same act, and one client review version decided twice opens one revision round. **A stale write is refused with the current row** (`expected_version`), so a page says what changed rather than overwriting it. `tests/ops.js` runs all of it against a real Postgres — 53 checks including a client contact with a real login and a database role granted select on every table reading zero tasks, and the audit trail refusing an edit and a delete. The canonical copy in `supabase/schema.sql` and the dated migration are compared byte for byte by that suite, because two copies of a data model drift the way two copies of a colour do. |
| Operations phase 2: My Work (`js/ops.js`, `supabase/migrations/2026-09-19-operations-phase2.sql`) | **The queue, and one task open.** `?s=work` in the address, **My Work** on the screen, `ops` in the ladder: the route is named for what a person does on it and the permission for what it governs, and the two are mapped once in `sectionAllowed()` rather than spelled in both vocabularies everywhere. **Three of phase 1's gates were data the page could not set**, so a task made in the console reached Ready and stopped: `ops_add_link` / `ops_set_link_archived` (the draft link Client review asks for and the final link Delivered asks for, a soft remove with Undo so a removal shuts the gate again), `ops_set_checklist` (a tick records who made it and an unticking stays on the record), `ops_set_video` (footage ready, which Editing is refused without; the row is created on demand and a key the payload does not carry is left as it stands, so ticking footage cannot blank a duration somebody typed). Each asks what every other write in section 6 asks and files its event in the same transaction; each ships in its own dated migration, mirrored in `supabase/schema.sql` under a second banner, and `tests/ops.js` compares **each** against **its own** migration, because a cut with no end marker takes everything appended to the file afterwards. **The queue is banded by when the work is owed** — Overdue, Due today, Due this week, Later, No date set, Finished — drawn by `js/group.js` like every other directory, with Later and Finished shut and the stage filter deciding whether Finished is drawn at all: a year of completed tasks under a band is a card nobody scrolls past, which is the filter's job and not the fold's. **A search opens every card; a stage filter does not**, because a search is somebody looking for one row and a filter is somebody choosing which work is on the page. **The count is read against the view somebody chose**, not against every row the database sent, or "Open work, mine" would print `5 of 6` on a screen nobody had filtered. **Whose work is a view, not a filter**: Clear the filters returns the search and the stage to the route's default and leaves the scope alone, and the scope select is offered only where `ops.all` is granted — which is the page agreeing with the database rather than deciding anything, since `ops_may_see_task` is what decides what arrives. **Mine is keyed on the owner's id, never their name**: two colleagues share a first name and a rename would quietly empty somebody's queue. **The task workspace is the `.rec` shape** the client and the campaign use: the task number as the mark (what an invoice, a message and a spreadsheet row all name it by; a monogram of a title says nothing), the title, the stage as a chip beside the ⋯, one derived line saying what it is waiting on, five panes (Overview, Checklist, Links, Time, Activity) and a rail (Stage, Your time, Key dates, People, Details). **One forward move is the action and the rest are a select beside it**, because eight buttons in a rail is eight things to read before doing the one that matters. **Which move is forward is the workflow's own to say** (`forwardOf()`: the stage nearest ahead by `position`, skipping the lanes beside the main line — blocked, waiting, kiv, cancelled — by `stage_group`): a hand-written preference list put Ready in front of Internal review on a task already In progress, because Ready is earlier in the workflow and the list did not know where the task was; and "not waiting" was the wrong test for the same job, because a review stage waits on a reviewer and is still on the main line. **Every refusal is named in the team's words** (`SAID`), never the database's: `Client review needs a draft or review link.`, not `needs-draft`. **A stale write repaints from the row the database handed back** rather than leaving the screen claiming something no longer true. **A checklist tick updates its own row and never repaints the list**, or the focus of somebody working down it with a keyboard is taken away by the tick they just made. **The timer is one open session a person across every task**: starting here stops the one running elsewhere, and the page names that task before the press. It counts in minutes, not seconds, because a second hand on a page somebody is working in is a distraction with no answer in it. **The way back is drawn where the act happened** (`.undobar-here` after the links list, naming the file that went), one bar at a time. `tests/work.js` drives all of it against `tests/stub2.js`, whose ops half mirrors every gate and every refusal, because a stand-in that cannot say no is one that cannot test a refusal. |
| My Work at volume (`js/ops.js`) | **Eight contents a week across thirty clients is about 290 tasks a month**, so the queue is built for that and not for the demo. **The read is bounded, and open work is not part of the bound**: a single `.limit(500)` over everything stopped showing older work in week seven and said nothing about it. Open work is read in full however old (a task overdue since August is the first thing the queue exists to show); finished work — the part that grows without limit — is read from the chosen period onward, in three plain filters rather than one `.or()`, because a cancelled task carries `cancelled_at` where a completed one carries `completed_at`. **The period select draws only while finished work can be on the page**, which is the only time it governs anything, and switching the stage filter into finished work runs the read again. **Group by is the axis** (`groupsOf()`): due date, client, stage or owner, the same rows and the same card asked a different question. **Off the due axis every card is shut**, because thirty open client cards are the 290 rows the axis exists to escape, and the heading then has to carry the overdue count itself (`marksOf()`) or it is hidden inside a shut card. **The fold is remembered per axis and the card keeps its own name on the page** (`ADspaceGroup.section`'s `memo`, added for this: `key` is the group's identity and `data-band`, `memo` is what the fold is filed under, defaulting to `key`). **A stage moves from the row** (`stageCell()` / `rowMove()`), which is the commonest act on the list, through `ops_transition_task` and the same gates, with the refusal named under the row it was made on (`.task-note`) rather than in a bar a screen away. Blocked is not offered there, because it needs a category first. **The row is therefore no longer one button**: a control inside a control is one a screen reader trips over, so the name cell (`.task-open`, the widest, full row height) is what opens the task and the chevron went with the button, a mark that was no longer a target. `tests/work.js` drives the period appearing and governing, each axis, the shut-by-default rule off the due axis, and a row move and its refusal. |
| Operations phase 3: three views and the bell (`js/ops.js`, `supabase/migrations/2026-09-19-operations-phase3.sql`) | **The same rows, asked three questions.** `view=` in the address (`board`, `calendar`; the list is the default and stays out of it), a List / Board / Calendar segment in the bar (`.cmdbar-views`), one read, one search, one filter, one scope for all three. **The board is one workflow at a time** (`#workWf` draws where Group by was, because the board has fixed that axis; the workflow with the most rows in view is chosen first): one column per stage in the workflow's own order, the count against the stage's `wip_guidance` (`4 / 5`, `.is-over` warn past it), the lanes beside the line (`SIDE`: blocked, waiting, kiv) gathered in one **On hold** column, a terminal stage drawn only while it holds rows. The bell (`#notifWrap`, in the console head before the account, drawn where `ops` is readable, wired from `ADspaceOps.signedIn()` which `applyAccess()` calls) counts the unread rows, lists them, opens the task a row names (the address written first, then `bridge.show('work')`) and marks it read, which is the one direct write to the table. **The bell is drawn in the Activity record's shape** (`.notif-btn` takes `.headlink`'s 15px glyph, mute ink and 8px `--line-soft` fill on hover and while open; the user asked on 2026-09-22 why the two differed): it had a 50% radius and a `--ink-mute` ring while its panel was open, which is the account avatar's circle borrowed for a control that is not one, and made the bell the only outlined thing in the bar. **The board's workflow select is filled before the empty state**, not inside `paintBoard()`: with no tasks `paint()` stops at `No tasks.` and the select stood where Group by had been with nothing in it, which the user reported as an empty record on 2026-09-22; `tests/stub2.js` keeps the same rule in `opsLog` and seeds two unread rows; `tests/work.js` drives the board, its WIP count, a refused move on a card, the calendar, the phone shapes, the capacity strip, the bell and the address round trip; the walk and the matrix carry the board, the calendar and the open bell. |
| Operations phase 4: the name, whose the work is, and the month (`supabase/migrations/2026-09-23-operations-phase4.sql`, `js/ops.js`, `js/crm.js`, 2026-09-23) | **Built from the user's brief of 2026-09-23 and the nine decisions taken with them (D1 to D9), on its own branch and PR, merged to `main` as #170 on 2026-09-23.** **A task is named by a code and a description.** The code is the database's, generated once under an advisory lock at creation and never rewritten: content month, planned publishing week and the client's running number for the month (`ops_code_of`: `2609W401` is September 2026, week 4, the client's first task that month; the number runs on across the month whatever week each lands in, so twelve over four weeks are 01 to 12). Inside a band the rows read by priority then date. **General and Video are retired for new tasks** (D8, `active = false`); the tasks on them carry on, and the board offers a retired workflow while any of its tasks is in view. **Duplicate, Generate, Repeat**: Duplicate in the task's ⋯ (`#dupSheet`: keep the description, copy the dates, copy the people, the month and week prefilled from the source; `ops_duplicate_task`, a new code, none of the history); **Generate** beside New task in the bar (`#genSheet`, behind the phone's ⋯ like the Documents bar's second action): a month of tasks for one client from a typed count, spread over four weeks or set per week in five figures that must add up, Preview reading the codes off the database's own next number and writing nothing (`ops_generate_month` dry run), the same sheet's second job running the month's repeat rules (`ops_generate_recurring`, idempotent on rule and date, so a second run makes nothing twice and says so); **Repeat** in the ⋯ (`#recSheet`: weekly, monthly on a day, or every so many days; ends on a date or after a count; the week the code takes; Stop repeating once a rule stands; `ops_set_recurring`, the rail's Details saying `Repeats Weekly until 28 Oct 2026`). **The engagement is one client's work for one month**, and it lives on the client record's **Work pane** (`data-pane="work"`, drawn by `ADspaceOps.clientWork` because the words, gates and sheets are My Work's and a second copy in `js/crm.js` would drift): a filter row (search, status, month, owner), then a card per month, this month open and older ones shut, the engagement's own facts at its head (manager, pieces planned, the Drive folder, the status as a state select, a pen for `#engSheet`), the content meeting line with `#meetSheet` (date today or later when first put in the diary, time, where it is held, who leads it, notes, or No meeting this month), and the thirteen readiness questions as a fold (`.engcheck`: the question, a state select over Not started / Waiting on client / In progress / Ready / Not applicable, an owner select; one row repaints on its own answer so a person working down the list keeps their place; the 150px state track is measured against `Waiting on client`, which the 124px state column clipped). **Ready and In production are claims the database checks** (`ops_engagement_set_status`: every check Ready or Not applicable and the meeting held or marked not applicable, refused in the team's words on the card), and a task's move into production waits on the same two facts. New task from the record prefills the client and the scope from its stage and joins the month's engagement where that month has one, and the task lands on the record's own list rather than carrying the person to My Work; opening a task from the pane writes the address first and then shows My Work, the way the bell does. The task rail carries an **Engagement** block (month, status, meeting, manager, `Open the month ›` to the record's Work pane). **Hand over** (D6) is an outline control beside the forward move (`#handSheet`: the next stage, the next person, a note; a stage past the next one says it skips a step and takes a reason): one call to `ops_transition_task` with `p_assignee` and `p_skip_reason`, so the owner ends and begins on the move and the record says `Handed to Aisyah at Ready for production`, the person is told, and every step passed over carries `stage_skipped` with who and why. | My Work is the team's daily task list (`js/ops.js`, `supabase/migrations/2026-09-24-my-work-daily-tasks.sql`, `-clear-test-tasks.sql`, 2026-09-24) | **The list stays, a task opens beside it, and the full record is one press further.** Built from the user's briefs "Reframe My Work as lightweight daily task tracking" and "Correct the My Work task structure", their decisions A to G, and the SOP they gave on 2026-09-23. **Views**: My day (the default, banded Overdue, Due today, In progress, Ready for review, Upcoming, No due date, Waiting, Completed today, with Completed today shut), Due today, Overdue, Due soon, Upcoming; **the views My Work was published with are back** (decision B): In progress, AQC review, Client review, Waiting on client, Scheduled and live, On hold or blocked, Late — keyed on `stage_group` and never on one workflow's stage key; the everyday task keeps its five words); the phone's short due words match the desk's (Today, Tomorrow). **The task number is `#WT00001`** (`ops_serial`, everywhere a number is printed), and an admin reads and sets the next one from **Task numbering** in the bar's ⋯ (`ops_next_task_no` / `ops_set_next_task_no`, refused at or below the highest in use, `ops.numbering` in the record). **The bar's ⋯** holds Bulk add (never "Generate"), From template, **Select tasks** (ops Manage: a tick on every row, a sticky bar counting them, Assign task owner and Delete; `ops_delete_tasks` takes the count typed back and a reason and files each deletion) and Task numbering. **The quick sheet** (`#taskDrawer`, `.sheet.sheet-side`, the portal's own sheet from the side and from the floor on a phone, over the list, from My Work, the bell or a client's Work pane; `open=` in the address): the number, the status and the title (edited where it sits, the code kept) at the head, then one card per part — the next step, the facts (Task Owner with Change, Due, Publish and Live where they apply, Created by, Priority as a select), Brief, Checklist, Files and links, Comments, Time and Recent activity (comments kept out of it). **One next action**, blue only where it hands the work to somebody else and ink for your own progress, with an unticked **Change Task Owner** on the step so the stage and the owner move in one press (`p_assignee`). **The SOP is the content workflow**: Planning, Ready to start, In progress, **AQC review** (Pass to client, or Request changes into **Revision (Internal)**), **Client review** (Mark approved, or Request changes into **Revision (Client)**), each review and revision counting its rounds, then Approved, **Scheduled** (the publish date), **Live** (the date confirmed, a reason where it differs from the scheduled one; `ops_mark_live`), **Performance review** three days on, handed back to whoever created the task with its checks, and **Completed** or **Taken down**, rated one to five (`ops_rate_task`). **A draft link is optional**: with none on the task the step reads **Sent on WhatsApp** and records it, and a row or board move into Client review without one opens the step sheet with that written in; the database accepts the link or a note. **Skipping a step is ops Work with a reason** (decision G), never into a revision. **Everything added can be put right and taken back**: checklist items (Rename, Remove, Undo; never a required check), links (Edit, Remove, Undo), comments (Edit, Delete, Undo, each a later event naming the comment so the history keeps every word), the brief and the priority (`ops_update_task`), on the full record as in the sheet (a link row there is Open and one ⋯ with Edit and Remove, never a row of icons); every stage move has **Revert** in the sheet's ⋯, with a reason. **A cancelled task can always be reopened**, as the cancel sheet promises: Reopen asks why and sends it back to the stage it was cancelled from where the workflow allows that, else to the workflow's own way out of Cancelled (Planning, To do, Intake), and the server now clears `cancelled_at` when a task leaves Cancelled (it had stayed set, so a reopened task went on reading Cancelled); a cancelled task's ring is empty rather than the green of finished work, and a cancelled everyday row has no tick. An open ⋯ answers Escape before the sheet does. **A step that changes a fact acts where the reader is** (`factHere()`): Assign task owner and Set due date open the sheet's own row while the sheet is open and the record's otherwise; they had reached for the record's controls from the sheet and done nothing. **The timer is off the default path** (the full record's ⋯). **The stage is read whole and keeps one place**: the row's stage track is 160px, measured against `Performance review` (157px at 12.5px), because the portal's 124px state column cut the SOP's names to "Performance r" and "Revision (Inter"; every `.state-select` ends a longer name in an ellipsis; a narrow row ends its Task Owner and date line with the stage at a stated width (160px, 200px under a finger), the Task Owner giving way with an ellipsis rather than the stage wrapping, and on a phone-width list (`is-tight`) the stage takes a line of its own on every row. **A list states its own narrow line** (`data-narrow="860"` on `#workQueue` and `#cwList`, read by `ADspaceState.fit`), because between 640 and 860 the six desk tracks left the task's name 64 to 108px; there the list takes the narrow row. | The operations report (`supabase/migrations/2026-09-21-operations-report.sql`, `js/ops.js`, 2026-09-21) | **The same work, asked an aggregate question.** Asked for by the user on 2026-09-21 ("a dashboard for admin / manager to see whats running, whats pending, whats on shooting … so during performance meeting, we'd be able to track and see our internal performance metrics"). A fourth view in My Work's segment (`view=report`), because the segment is already "the same rows asked a different question" and a ninth route would break the rail's two chunks of four. **One function, one read, five answers** (`ops_report(p_from, p_to)`): what is running, what is late, how long each stage takes, whether the work was there on time, and the same per person. **It adds no table, column, policy or permission** — every figure is derived from `ops_tasks`, `ops_workflow_stages` and the `ops_task_events` rows written since phase 1, which is the whole reason the events are append-only; there is nothing to backfill. **`ops.reports` governs it**, a part that has existed in the ladder since phase 1 and is granted and never inherited, so a group given `ops: work` is not quietly handed the team's numbers; the button is hidden where it is not held **and `view=report` in the address falls back to the list**, because a link somebody was sent must not open a view the database would only deny. **Median, not mean, and never a bare figure**: one task stuck in Editing for three months moves a mean enough to say nothing about the ordinary case, so each stage carries its median, its 90th percentile and the count it was taken over. **Replanning is counted beside the on-time rate and never inside it** — an extension would otherwise erase the miss it was granted for, and a rate that cannot be missed measures nothing. **By person is the foundation of a KPI and not a KPI**: what somebody finished, how much was on time, how long it took end to end, with no score and no ranking. **The report goes through `paint()` and never straight to `paintReport()`**, because `viewBox()` is what hides the queue, the board and the calendar — drawn directly it left the list on the screen underneath. The list's own controls leave with it (a search over an aggregate filters nothing, a stage filter over "what is running" is the question), and the period is always drawn there because it is the window every figure is taken over. **My Work reached the audit walk for the first time with it**: the route was built across three phases and never added, so its queue, its views and this report had never had their alignment, contrast or touch targets measured — and the walk failed on the first run, on a phone template whose `.is-narrow .rep-row` lost to `.is-narrow .svc-row` later in the file and left four tracks whose last was sized by its own content, so two rows started their figures 7px apart. Measured (`241px 0px 0px 48.8px`), not guessed, and moved rather than fought with specificity. `tests/ops.js` proves the arithmetic against real Postgres — that running counts every open task once, that met plus missed is what reached review, that the late list matches the same rule asked in SQL, and that ops Work alone is refused until `ops.reports` is granted — and `tests/work.js` drives the view, its controls and the address. |
| The task record, once over (`js/ops.js`, 2026-09-22) | **Every fact on the task record is stated once, and the number is a token, not a disc.** The user sent three things back on 2026-09-22: the serial (`T1002` pressed into the 46px `.rec-mark` disc, a shape for a logo or a monogram, read as a badly fitted logo), an owner change that saved with nothing on the screen saying so, and a rail whose forward move was a blue slab the width of the rail. **The head has no mark** (`.rec-id-plain`: `"token ctl" / "who ctl"` at a desk, `"token ctl" / "who who"` on a phone so only the number shares its line with the chip and the ⋯ and the title takes the card's width): the number is the first line of who this is, in the token face, and a copy control (`.serial-copy.rec-token`, `ADspaceCopy.to`) for the reason the Register's serial is one. **The rail's move is a button at its own width** (`.railmove { width: auto }`) and the rail no longer repeats the stage chip the head carries. **The dates are in the rail and nowhere else** (Key dates, with `moved from {original}` under a commitment that moved, the three closing timestamps once they exist, and `Move a date` under them on the dates' own margin); the Overview's Dates section, which printed the same three dates a second time on one screen, is gone. **The owner is on the identity line and in the select that changes it, and nowhere else**: the rail's read-only Owner row was the same fact a third time, so People names only the reviewer and the contributors and its select is labelled `Owner` again. **Number** left Details, because the head is the number. **The owner control answers**: `Saved.` under it on success, `No change.` when the pick is the owner already held, and on a refusal the select is put back to the owner the database still holds, because it already showed the new name before anything was saved and a refused save left the screen claiming a change that had not happened; `state.owners` / `state.ownerIds` are written on success so the queue behind the record agrees without a second read. **The Overview is Brief, Video where there is one, Progress and Links** (the live links with `Manage links ›` / `Add a link ›` opening the pane, the shape the client record's Overview gives its letters), and the rail gains **Recent activity** (the last three events, read with the task, `.raillog-plain` with no section glyph because a task's events are all the task's). `tests/stub2.js` can refuse any function by name (`window.__refuseRpc = { ops_assign_task: 'denied' }`), and `tests/work.js` drives the token, the once-only rule for the number, the dates and the owner, the move's width, the owner save saying Saved, and the refusal putting the select back. |
| Whose task it is (`js/ops.js`, 2026-09-22) | **The owner was blank everywhere, and it was one embed.** `ops_task_assignees` points at `team_members` twice (`team_member_id` and `assigned_by`) and so does `ops_work_sessions` (`team_member_id` and `corrected_by`), so PostgREST refused `team_members(name)` outright with PGRST201 and the three reads that asked for it returned nothing at all: no owner on any row, on any card or on the record; **Mine** matched nothing because it keys on `state.ownerIds`; the capacity strip counted no open work; and the record's owner select fell back to **Nobody** the moment `readTask` came back — a moment after the save that had in fact worked printed `Saved.` The foreign key is named now (`team_members!ops_task_assignees_team_member_id_fkey(name)`), **a refused read of who owns what fails the list and the record rather than drawing an unowned task**, and `tests/stub2.js` refuses a bare ambiguous embed the way PostgREST does (`TWO_WAYS`), because a stand-in more generous than the database is one that cannot see this class of fault. | The stage says where the task is (`js/ops.js`, 2026-09-22) | **Four families and no more.** `stageTone` was terminal-or-waiting, so Intake, Ready, In progress, Shooting, Editing, Revision, Approved and Delivered all drew the same neutral select and nothing on the row said where the task had got to. It keys on `stage_group`, which the workflow already carries: not started is mute (`intake`, `kiv`, `cancelled`), the work in hand carries no paint at all because it is the ordinary case, anything waiting on a person is warn (`internal_review`, `client_review`, `waiting`), anything cleared is green (`approved`, `delivered`, `done`), and blocked is `--err` — which the select had no rule for at all. | The board is shorter, and a card is dragged (`js/ops.js`, 2026-09-20) | **Nine columns of which seven said None is a board nobody scrolls.** The user asked on 2026-09-20 whether the long sideways row was necessary, and asked why a card could not be dragged. **An empty column is drawn only where the work can still go**: a stage something on this board could be moved into next (`reach`, off the `next_stage_keys` of the stages that hold rows), or the workflow's own entry, because a board with nowhere to start reads as a board missing its first step. Everything else leaves and comes back by itself the moment a task can reach it; **On hold is drawn only while something is on hold**, since it is where work steps *out* of the flow and an empty one is a column for a thing that has not happened. The video workflow draws five columns instead of nine on the fixture. **A card is dragged into a column** and it is the same move as the select on it: `moveTo()` is the one path for the list row's select, the card's select and a drop alike, so all three go through `ops_transition_task`, the same gates and the same refusal named on the thing that was moved. **The board says where the card may go before it is dropped** (`.is-shut-out` on every column the workflow does not allow, `.is-drop` on the one that would take it), which is error prevention rather than an error message. **On hold is not a drop target** (`noDrop`), because Blocked needs a category first and that is asked for on the record. **A finger drags too, from a grip** (2026-09-20, the user asked why the card could not be moved on a phone): HTML5 drag and drop fires nothing under a touch, so the whole drag is **pointer events** now and is one path for both inputs. A phone decides whether a touch is a scroll the moment it lands, so a press and hold was cancelled the instant the finger moved and the card never lifted; the handle is what fixes that, because `touch-action: none` declared **on the grip itself** means the browser never claims a gesture starting there while the rest of the card goes on scrolling the board. `.bcard-grip` is drawn at every width (a control that appears only on a phone is one somebody discovers twice) at 44px under a finger; at a desk the whole card is a handle too, since a mouse has no scroll to lose. What follows the hand is a **clone** (`.bcard-ghost`, `position: fixed`, `pointer-events: none`), because taking the real card out of its column reflows the board mid gesture and because `elementFromPoint` is how the column under the finger is found. A column advertises itself with `data-drop` and one handler reads it, rather than every column carrying its own listeners. **The select stays on every card**, because a keyboard has no drag. |

The serial is 11.5px, weight 500, mute, first in the meta line with the client; the title is 14px/600 ink and is what opens the task; the stage select sits at the same place on every card; the foot is the owner as a 20px initials disc and a name (a dashed empty disc and `Unassigned` where there is none) with the due date on the right, warn once it has passed. |

The copy is **one sentence a route**, official register: the first pass ran to two and named the panes inside a record, which explains the product rather than the section. |
| The rail's order, and everything that repeats it (`admin/index.html`, `js/admin.js`, `js/team.js`) | **Eight routes in two chunks of four, ordered by how often each is opened, and the same sequence everywhere it appears.** Asked for by the user on 2026-09-20 against the serial position effect. **Work**: My Work, Clients, Content Review, Creator Campaigns. **Records and setup**: Documents, Short Links, Services, Team. The two ends are what the effect actually governs: the first slot is found fastest and goes to the queue somebody opens on arrival and returns to all day, the last is the second most findable and goes to the settings-like route every convention puts there, and what gets lost in the middle is then what is opened by an event rather than by habit — which is the right thing to lose. The groups are Miller's chunk (`.navgroup`, a 12px block step between them, the existing `.sidebar-label` face) and a group whose every route is withheld hides its heading with it (`applyAccess`), because a label over nothing is a table header over no rows. **The axis is not client-facing against internal**, which the user proposed and which does not divide this console: every route here is internal and four of them produce something a client eventually sees. **Clients leaving the first slot is a deliberate change to a documented decision** ("Clients first; everything hangs off a client"): that sentence is about the data model, which is unchanged, and was never a claim about frequency. The same order now drives `SECTIONS` in `js/admin.js`, the Activity record's tab strip and `ACT_SECTION`, `PARTS.activity`, and the Team panel's section blocks and its activity parts, so a person learns one sequence and not four. |
| Two dates, two authorities (`supabase/migrations/2026-09-21-draft-date-is-the-teams-own.sql`, `js/ops.js`, 2026-09-21) | **The first draft date is the team's own and the final date is the client's.** Asked for by the user on 2026-09-21. The **first draft** date is an internal milestone set against a schedule already agreed, so it is freely adjustable by anybody who may work the task and raises no ask: `ops_due_decider(p_task, p_kind)` answers **null** for `first_draft`, so `ops_request_due_change` falls straight through to the move. That closes the item this repository carried as open since 2026-09-20, where both files recorded an intent the shipped function did not implement and `tests/ops.js` asserted the code while flagging the difference; the user chose, so the intent moved into the code. The **final** date keeps the approval round. **A draft on or after the final date is a plan that cannot work**: `ops_due_order_ok(draft, final)` compares **calendar days** (a timestamp comparison would refuse a draft set for the morning of the day before a midnight final, which works perfectly), the latest a draft may fall is the day before, and the check sits inside `ops_change_due_date` as well as `ops_request_due_change` — `ops_decide_due_change` reaches the move directly once an extension is approved, so a gate only on the asking path is one an approval walks past. It needed no edit: it already puts its decision back when the move refuses. **The page draws two controls, not one word covering both** (`#taskDraftDate` and `#taskDateMove`, outlined `.btn-sm` in a wrapping row): **Set draft date** / **Change draft date**, and **Request extension** where the task is somebody else's or **Change due date** where it is yours. **The word "Move" is gone from every date control and from the sheet's title**, which the user had asked for once before and which was missed; the quiet `btn-quiet` text button it replaced read as a sentence of information rather than as something to press, and it only ever opened the **final** kind, so the draft date was unreachable from the rail at all. The sheet states the ceiling in words and the picker carries `max` as the day before the commitment, which is error prevention rather than an error message, and `draft-not-before-final` is named in the team's words like every other refusal. **The stand-in read `p_when` where the page sends `p_value`**, so every date move in a test wrote `undefined` and still reported success; it reads `p_value` now and refuses the order the way the database does. `tests/ops.js` drives the ungated draft, both refusals, the day-before boundary and the final date's round against real Postgres; `tests/work.js` drives the two controls, the absent word, the titles, the cap and the named refusal. **The final date's round is confirmed, not assumed** (the user, 2026-09-21, asked the same day this shipped): "if changes needed it requires the approval flow back to the original created owner". So the reading here is the settled one — the final date cannot be moved by the person doing the work, and the round back to the creator is the only way it moves. **And the date means a stage, not a square on a calendar**: the final due date is the day the work is owed *at client review*, so a task that has not reached Client review by then is late whatever its stage says. That flag and who sees it are the next batch, with the dashboard. |
| An extension is asked for, not taken (`supabase/migrations/2026-09-20-extension-approval.sql`, `js/ops.js`) | **A commitment is the promise the person who created the task made, so moving it is theirs to allow.** `ops_change_due_date` moved the date the moment somebody pressed it, and the person who set it found out afterwards, from a notification, about a deadline that had already moved. The user gave the flow on 2026-09-20: A creates the task and assigns it to B; B's ask goes back to A. `ops_due_requests` holds one open ask a task and a kind (a partial unique index, so a second press is the same ask), read-only to a browser like every other `ops_` table, so **nobody approves their own extension whatever they send**. `ops_request_due_change` is the **one call the page makes**, and the database decides what it is: a person moving a date on a task they created falls straight through to `ops_change_due_date`, because a round with one name on both ends is a form and not a control. Everybody else raises the ask, which notifies the creator in the team's words and files `due_requested`. `ops_decide_due_change` is the only path to the moved date, so the original-commitment rule and the `due_changed` event are the same whether or not an approval was needed; a gate that refuses **after** the approval puts the decision back rather than recording a yes against a date that never moved. **ops Manage may also decide**, because somebody has to when the creator has left, and the person who asked never can. `ops_withdraw_due_change` is the way back while it is open. **The first draft date was meant not to be gated** — it is the team's own milestone and the commitment a client is owed is the final date — **and the shipped function gates it like the final one**: `ops_due_decider()` answers with the task's creator whatever the kind, so both dates go through the same round. On the page the control is named for what pressing it will do (**Request extension** where the task is somebody else's, **Move a date** where it is yours), it leaves while an ask is open, and the rail draws the ask under the dates it is about with the one action the reader has — Approve and Decline for the person asked, Withdraw for the one who asked, and nothing at all for anybody else. **The reason category is a stored key and the record printed it** (`client_request` under a sheet offering Client request); `reasonWord()` names all six once, with sentence case as the fallback, and every place a reason is shown reads it. `tests/ops.js` drives fourteen rules against real Postgres and `tests/work.js` the page, including that asking does not move the date. |
| A quality check before a client sees the work (`js/campaigns.js` `QC_CHECKS`, `#qcSheet`) | **Release to client is the only move on a creator's card a client sees the moment it is made, and it was one press.** Asked for by the user on 2026-09-20.
